.
We obtain the commuting squares for
T_{D}
by flipping
the 7 commuting squares obtained in Step 3 along the diagonal.

To recover the infinite path space associated to
T_{\Lambda_{I}}
from the above four Johnson–Madden moves, we will delete the edges
\lambda_{k}^{j^{i}}\in F_{D}
where
i\neq j
.
Note that this procedure converts
F_{D}
into
F_{\Lambda_{I}}
.

As further evidence that the conjugacy
{\sf X}_{T_{I}}^{+}\cong{\sf X_{D}}^{+}
guaranteed by Theorem
6.1
does not arise from an isomorphism of textile systems, however, note that we do not have
E_{D}\cong E_{\Lambda_{I}}
, as
E_{\Lambda_{I}}
has three vertices and
E_{D}
has two.

7.
When textile-system insplitting yields a 2-graph insplitting

In the previous section, we showed in Theorem
6.1
how to reconstruct a 2-graph insplitting via four Johnson–Madden moves. In this section, we identify several other perspectives on the relationship between 2-graph insplitting and Johnson–Madden insplitting. We first show (Theorem
7.1
) that, alternatively, the 2-graph insplitting may be reconstructed via a single Johnson–Madden insplit, together with an insplitting of the base graph
E
of the textile system. Then, Theorem
7.3
shows that certain Johnson–Madden insplits yield 2-graph insplits, and Theorem
7.12
establishes
that certain insplits of the base graph
E
of a textile system yield both Johnson–Madden insplits and 2-graph insplits. Finally, Section
7.4
reveals the compatibility of these perspectives.

These three theorems
can be summarized by the diagrams below, where the solid arrows indicate relationships that are assumed (or known in general), and the dotted arrows indicate relationships which are constructed in the theorem.

T
Theorem
7.1
F
E
\Lambda
\widetilde{T}
\widetilde{F}
\widetilde{E}
\Lambda_{I}
T
Theorem
7.3
F
E
\Lambda
\widetilde{T}
\widetilde{F}
\widetilde{E}
\Lambda_{I}
Hypotheses (1)&(2)
T
Theorem
7.12
F
E
\Lambda
\widetilde{T}
\widetilde{F}
\widetilde{E}
\Lambda_{I}
Hyp. of Thm.
7.12

The results in Section
7.4
confirm that the diagrams above can be superimposed on each other; or, equivalently, that any one of the horizontal arrows in the diagram determines the others. For example, the dotted arrow
F\to\widetilde{F}
in the first diagram (which is constructed in Theorem
7.1
) satisfies the hypothesis in Theorem
7.3
.

7.1.
Textile insplitting from 2-graph insplitting

While Theorem
6.1
shows that a 2-graph insplit can be recovered by four Johnson–Madden moves, we establish in the following Theorem that we could alternatively recover the 2-graph insplitting by combining a single Johnson–Madden insplitting with an insplit of the base graph
E
of the textile system.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
T=(p,q:F\to E)
be an LR textile system. Let
\Lambda
denote the 2-graph associated to
T
and assume that
\Lambda
can be
(2-graph) insplit using the partition
\{\mathcal{G}^{j}_{z}:z\in E^{0},1\leq j\leq m(z)\}
of
\Lambda^{1}
.
For each
v\in F^{0}
, define

(21)

\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}:=\{\lambda\in F^{1}:r(\lambda)=v,\;\;p(\lambda)\in\mathcal{G}^{i}_{r(p(\lambda))=p(v)}\},

and for
z\in E^{0}
, define

(22)

\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}:=\mathcal{G}^{i}_{z}\cap zE^{1}.

Denote by
\widetilde{F}
the directed graph resulting from directed-graph insplitting on
F
at every vertex
v\in F^{0}
, using the partition
\{\mathcal{F}_{v}^{i}:1\leq i\leq m(p(v)),v\in F^{0}\}.

Let
\widetilde{E}
denote the directed graph resulting from directed-graph insplitting on
E
at each vertex
z\in E^{0}
, using the partition
\{\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}:1\leq i\leq m(z),z\in E^{0}\}.

Define maps
\widetilde{p},\widetilde{q}:\widetilde{F}\to\widetilde{E}
by

\displaystyle\widetilde{p}(\lambda^{i})

\displaystyle:=p(\lambda)^{i},\ \widetilde{q}(\lambda^{i}):=q(\lambda)^{m},\mbox{ where }s(\lambda)\in\mathcal{G}^{m}_{q(s(\lambda))},

\displaystyle\widetilde{p}(v^{i})

\displaystyle:=p(v)^{i},\ \widetilde{q}(v^{i}):=q(v)^{m},\mbox{ where }v\in\mathcal{G}^{m}_{q(v)},

where
\lambda\in F^{1}
,
v\in F^{0}
. Then

(1)

The maps
\widetilde{p},\widetilde{q}:\widetilde{F}\to\widetilde{E}
are graph homomorphisms.

(2)

\widetilde{T}:=(\widetilde{p},\widetilde{q}:\widetilde{F}\to\widetilde{E})
is an LR textile system.

(3)

The 2-graph associated to
\widetilde{T}
(denoted by
\widetilde{\Lambda}
) is identical to the 2-graph
{\Lambda}_{I}
resulting from insplitting
\Lambda
using the partition
\{\mathcal{G}^{j}_{z}:z\in E^{0},1\leq j\leq m(z)\}
.

Proof.

To see (1),
we will check that
r(\widetilde{p}(\lambda^{i}))=\widetilde{p}(r(\lambda^{i}))
and that
r(\widetilde{q}(\lambda^{i}))=\widetilde{q}(r(\lambda^{i}))
for all
\lambda^{i}\in\widetilde{F}^{1}
. (The checks that
s\circ\widetilde{p}=\widetilde{p}\circ s
and
s\circ\widetilde{q}=\widetilde{q}\circ s
are straightforward computations which we leave to the reader.)
For the first computation, if
\lambda\in\mathcal{F}^{j}_{r(\lambda)}
then
r(\lambda^{i})=r(\lambda)^{j}
and
p(\lambda)\in\mathcal{G}^{j}_{p(r(\lambda))}
. If
r(p(\lambda))(=p(r(\lambda)))=z\in E^{0}
, then when we insplit
E
we have
r(p(\lambda)^{k})=z^{j}
for all
k
. Therefore,

r(\widetilde{p}(\lambda^{i}))=r(p(\lambda)^{i})=z^{j}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\widetilde{p}(r(\lambda^{i}))=\widetilde{p}(r(\lambda)^{j})=z^{j},

as desired.

To see that
r(\widetilde{q}(\lambda^{i}))=\widetilde{q}(r(\lambda^{i}))
, observe that since
\mathcal{G}
is a 2-graph insplitting partition, we will have
r(\lambda)
and
q(\lambda)
in the same partition set
\mathcal{G}^{n}_{r(q(\lambda))}
. Therefore, regardless of which
m
satisfies
s(\lambda)\in\mathcal{G}^{m}_{q(s(\lambda))}
, we have

r(\widetilde{q}(\lambda^{i}))=r(q(\lambda)^{m})=r(q(\lambda))^{n},

and regardless of which
\ell
satisfies
\lambda\in\mathcal{F}^{\ell}_{r(\lambda)}
, we have

\widetilde{q}(r(\lambda^{i}))=\widetilde{q}(r(\lambda)^{\ell})=q(r(\lambda))^{n}=r(q(\lambda))^{n}
,
since
q
is a graph homomorphism. We conclude that
\widetilde{p},\widetilde{q}
are graph homomorphisms, as claimed.

For (2), to see that
\widetilde{T}
is an LR textile system, we first observe that the function
\widetilde{F}^{1}\ni\lambda^{i}\mapsto(r(\lambda^{i}),\widetilde{p}(\lambda^{i}),s(\lambda^{i}),\widetilde{q}(\lambda^{i}))\in\widetilde{F}^{0}\times\widetilde{E}^{1}\times\widetilde{F}^{0}\times\widetilde{E}^{1}
is injective, because if
r(\lambda^{i})=r(\mu^{j})
then
r(\lambda)=r(\mu)=:v
and
\lambda,\mu\in\mathcal{F}^{n}_{v}
lie in the same partition set. Furthermore, if
\widetilde{p}(\lambda^{i})=\widetilde{p}(\mu^{j})
then
p(\lambda)^{i}=p(\mu)^{j}
so we must have
p(\mu)=p(\lambda)
and
i=j
. Since
T
is LR,
r(\lambda)=r(\mu)
and
p(\lambda)=p(\mu)
, we conclude that
\lambda=\mu
. As
i=j
we also have
\lambda^{i}=\mu^{j}
, so the function is injective as claimed and
\widetilde{T}
is a textile system.

To see that
\widetilde{T}
is LR, observe that if
\widetilde{p}(v^{i})=r(e^{j})\in\widetilde{E}^{0}
, then the definitions of the insplitting
\widetilde{E}
and the homomorphism
\widetilde{p}
imply that we have
p(v)^{i}=r(e)^{n}
where
e\in\mathcal{E}^{n}_{r(e)}
. In particular, we have
p(v)=r(e)
and
i=n
. Since the textile system
T
is LR, there exists a unique
\lambda\in F^{1}
with
p(\lambda)=e
and
r(\lambda)=v
. As
p(\lambda)=e\in\mathcal{G}^{i}_{p(v)}
, we have
\lambda\in\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}
, and so
r(\lambda^{j})=v^{i}
. The uniqueness of
\lambda
with
p(\lambda)=e
and
r(\lambda)=v
implies that
\lambda^{j}\in\widetilde{F}^{1}
is the unique edge with
\widetilde{p}(\lambda^{j})=e^{j}
and
r(\lambda^{j})=v^{i}
. That is,
\widetilde{p}
is left resolving.
To check that
\widetilde{q}
is right resolving, suppose that
\widetilde{q}(v^{i})=s(e^{m})\in\widetilde{E}^{0}
for some
e^{m}\in\widetilde{E}^{1}
. As
s(e^{m})=s(e)^{m}
in
\widetilde{E}^{0}
, the definition of
\widetilde{q}
implies that
s(e)=q(v)
, and moreover that
v\in\mathcal{G}^{m}_{q(v)}
. Since
T
is LR, i.e.
q
is right resolving, there exists a unique
\lambda\in F^{1}
such that
q(\lambda)=e
and
s(\lambda)=v
.
For any
1\leq j\leq m(p(r(\lambda)))
, we will then have
\widetilde{q}(\lambda^{j})=q(\lambda)^{m}=e^{m}
and
s(\lambda^{j})=s(\lambda)^{j}=v^{j}
. That is, there is a unique edge
f
in
\widetilde{F}^{1}
with source
v^{i}
and
q(f)=e^{m}
, namely,
f=\lambda^{i}
. We conclude that
\widetilde{q}
is right resolving, and so

\widetilde{T}
is LR.

For (3), we claim that the 2-graph
\Lambda_{I}
resulting from 2-graph insplitting
\Lambda

according to the partition
\{\mathcal{G}^{i}_{z}:z\in E^{0},1\leq i\leq m(z)\}

is identical to the graph
\widetilde{\Lambda}
associated to the textile system
\widetilde{T}
.
To see this, we first recall that since each set
\mathcal{G}^{j}_{z}
contains at least one edge of each color, each set
\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}
is nonempty. Thus,

\widetilde{\Lambda}^{0}=\widetilde{E}^{0}=\{z^{i}:z\in E^{0},1\leq i\leq m(z)\}.

Similarly,
\Lambda_{I}^{0}=\{z^{i}:z\in E^{0},1\leq i\leq m(z)\},

so
\Lambda_{I}^{0}=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{0}.

Next, note that
\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\varepsilon_{1}}=\widetilde{E}^{1}=\{e^{j}:e\in E^{1},1\leq j\leq m(s(e))\},
while

\Lambda_{I}^{\varepsilon_{1}}=\{e^{i}:e\in\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{1}},1\leq i\leq m(s(e))\}=\{e^{i}:e\in E^{1},1\leq i\leq m(s(e))\}=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\varepsilon_{1}}.

As
s_{\Lambda}(v)=p(v)
, we also have
\Lambda_{I}^{\varepsilon_{2}}=\{v^{i}:v\in F^{0},1\leq i\leq m(s_{\Lambda}(v))\}
, while

\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\varepsilon_{2}}=\{v^{j}:v\in F^{0},1\leq j\leq m(p(v))\}=\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{2}}_{I}.

Moreover, the range and source maps are the same in
\Lambda_{I}
as in
\widetilde{\Lambda}
. To see this, we first observe that for each
e^{i}\in\widetilde{E}^{1}=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\varepsilon_{1}}=\Lambda_{I}^{\varepsilon_{1}},
we have
r_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}(e^{i})=r_{\widetilde{E}}(e)=r(e)^{j}
if
e\in\mathcal{E}^{j}_{r(e)}\subseteq\mathcal{G}^{j}_{r(e)}
, while
r_{\Lambda_{I}}(e^{i})=r(e)^{j}
. Similarly,
s_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}(e^{i})=s_{\widetilde{E}}(e^{i})=s_{E}(e)^{i}
, and
s_{\Lambda_{I}}(e^{i})=s_{\Lambda}(e)^{i}=s_{E}(e)^{i}
. For
v^{i}\in\widetilde{F}^{0}
, we also have
s_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}(v^{i})=\widetilde{p}(v^{i})=p(v)^{i}
, whereas
s_{\Lambda_{I}}(v^{i})=s_{\Lambda}(v)^{i}=p(v)^{i}
. Finally, if
v\in\mathcal{G}^{m}_{q(v)},

r_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}(v^{i})=\widetilde{q}(v^{i})=q(v)^{m}
and
r_{\Lambda_{I}}(v^{i})=r_{\Lambda}(v)^{m}=q(v)^{m}
since
v\in\mathcal{G}^{m}_{q(v)}
.

It remains to check that the factorization rules are the same in
\Lambda_{I}
as in
\widetilde{\Lambda}
. To that end, suppose that

w^{k}f^{\ell}
is a composable 2-color path in
\Lambda_{I}
with
w^{k}\in\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{2}}_{I},f^{\ell}\in\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{1}}_{I}
. As
\Lambda_{I}
is a 2-graph by Theorem
5.10
, there is a unique composable path
e^{i}v^{j}
with
e^{i}v^{j}\sim_{\Lambda_{I}}w^{k}f^{\ell}.

Since
s_{\Lambda_{I}}(w^{k})=s_{\Lambda}(w)^{k}=p(w)^{k}
, we must have
f\in\mathcal{G}^{k}_{r(f)=p(w)}
. Since
r(f)=p(w)
and
p
is left resolving, there is a unique
\lambda\in F^{1}
with
p(\lambda)=f,r(\lambda)=w
.
If
e^{i}v^{j}\sim_{\Lambda_{I}}w^{k}f^{\ell}
, then in particular
ev\sim_{\Lambda}wf
. That is, the unique
\lambda\in F^{1}
which satisfies
p(\lambda)=f,r(\lambda)=w
also has
q(\lambda)=e,s(\lambda)=v
(and vice versa). Moreover, as
s_{\Lambda_{I}}(v^{j})=p(v)^{j}
and
s_{\Lambda_{I}}(f^{\ell})=s(f)^{\ell}
, we must have
j=\ell.

Now, consider
\sim_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}
. We will show that we also have
w^{k}f^{\ell}\sim_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}e^{i}v^{\ell}
. Since the source and range maps are the same in
\widetilde{\Lambda}
and in
\Lambda_{I}
, we know that
w^{k}f^{\ell}
is composable in
\widetilde{\Lambda}
, that is,
r_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}(f^{\ell})=s_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}(w^{k})=\widetilde{p}(w^{k})
. In other words,
f\in\mathcal{G}^{k}_{p(w)}
. As we established when we checked (as part of Statement (2)) that
\widetilde{p}
was left resolving, there is a unique
\lambda^{\ell}\in\widetilde{F}^{1}
with
\widetilde{p}(\lambda^{\ell})=f^{\ell}=p(\lambda)^{\ell}
and
r(\lambda^{\ell})=w^{k}.

In particular,
\lambda\in F^{1}
satisfies
p(\lambda)=f
and
r(\lambda)=w
, so
q(\lambda)=e
and
s(\lambda)=v
for the
e,v
discussed in the previous paragraph. That is, we have
v\in\mathcal{G}^{i}_{s(e)}.

Thus,
w^{k}f^{\ell}\sim_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}e^{i}v^{\ell}
, since
v\in\mathcal{G}^{i}_{s(e)}
implies that

r_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}(v^{\ell})=\widetilde{q}(v^{\ell})=q(v)^{i}=s(e)^{i}=s(e^{i}).

As both
\widetilde{\Lambda}
and
\Lambda_{I}
are 2-graphs (thanks to Theorems
4.6
and
5.10
respectively), every blue-red path is equivalent to a unique red-blue path. The fact that any 2-color path
w^{k}f^{\ell}
is equivalent to the same path
e^{i}v^{\ell}
under both equivalence relations implies that
\widetilde{\Lambda}=\Lambda_{I}
as claimed.

∎

Example 7.2
.

Consider again the 2-graph
\Lambda
associated to the textile system from Example
6.2
:

As we observed in Equation (

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{v}^{1}=\{f_{1},e_{1}\},\,\mathcal{G}_{w}^{1}=\{f_{2},e_{2}\},\,\mathcal{G}_{w}^{2}=\{f_{3},e_{3}\},

then the partition
\mathcal{G}=\{\mathcal{G}_{v}^{1},\mathcal{G}_{w}^{1},\mathcal{G}_{w}^{2}\}
of
\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{1}}\sqcup\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{2}}

to this partition, we see that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{v}^{1}=\{e_{1}\},

\displaystyle=\{\lambda_{1}\},\quad\mathcal{F}_{f_{2}}^{1}=\{\lambda_{2}\},\quad\mathcal{F}_{f_{3}}^{1}=\{\lambda_{3}\},\quad\mathcal{F}_{f_{3}}^{2}=\{\lambda_{4}\}.

, and

7.2.
Which textile insplits yield 2-graph insplits?

The following Theorem explains how to identify when a Johnson–Madden insplitting of an LR textile system could alternatively be obtained from a 2-graph insplitting. In other words, while we know from Theorem
5.6
that Johnson–Madden insplitting never yields an LR textile system, Theorem
7.3
identifies which Johnson–Madden insplittings of an LR textile system can be combined with a directed-graph insplitting of the base graph
E
to yield an LR textile system.
Thus, the insplittings described in the theorem below take an LR textile system to an LR textile system.

Theorem 7.3
.

Let
T=(p,q:F\to E)
be an LR textile system in which
p
is surjective and
F
is source-free, and let
\Lambda=\Lambda_{T}
be the corresponding 2-graph. Suppose for each
v\in F^{0}
we have a partition
\mathcal{F}_{v}=\{\mathcal{F}_{v}^{1},\dots,\mathcal{F}_{v}^{m(v)}\}
of
vF^{1}
, and let
\mathcal{F}=\{\mathcal{F}_{v}\}_{v\in F^{0}}
. Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
satisfies

(1)

If
v,w\in F^{0}
and
p(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v})\cap p(\mathcal{F}^{j}_{w})\not=\emptyset
, then
p(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v})=p(\mathcal{F}^{j}_{w});
and

(2)

For all
v\in F^{0}
, there exists
w\in F^{0}
and
1\leq j\leq m(w)
such that
q(vF^{1})\subseteq p(\mathcal{F}^{j}_{w})
.

Let
T_{JM}=(p_{JM},q_{JM}:F_{JM}\to E_{JM})
be the result of performing Johnson–Madden insplitting with respect to the partition
\mathcal{F}
. Then there is a directed graph insplit
\widetilde{E}
of
E_{JM}
, together with graph homomorphisms
\widetilde{p},\widetilde{q}:F_{JM}\to\widetilde{E}
, so that the textile system
\widetilde{T}:=(\widetilde{p},\widetilde{q}:F_{JM}\to\widetilde{E})
is LR.

Moreover,
\widetilde{T}
can be identified as the textile system
T_{\Lambda_{I}}
of a
2-graph
\Lambda_{I}
which arises from 2-graph insplitting on
\Lambda

using a partition
\mathcal{G}
(described in Proposition
7.9
below) of
\Lambda^{1}
which arises from
\mathcal{F}.

Theorem
7.3
will be proved in a series of propositions.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
T=(p,q:F\to E)
be an LR textile system, and for each
v\in F^{0}
,
\mathcal{F}_{v}=\{\mathcal{F}_{v}^{i}:i=1,\dots,m(v)\}
be a partition of
vF^{1}
. Then:

(1)

if
v\in F^{0}
and
p(\mathcal{F}_{v}^{i})\cap p(\mathcal{F}_{v}^{j})\neq\emptyset
, then
i=j
, and

(2)

if
v,w\in F^{0}
and
p(v)=p(w)
, then for every
i\in\{1,\dots,m(v)\}
, there exists
j\in\{1,\dots,m(w)\}
such that
p(\mathcal{F}_{v}^{i})\cap p(\mathcal{F}_{w}^{j})\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

(
1
): Let
e\in p(\mathcal{F}_{v}^{i})\cap p(\mathcal{F}_{v}^{j})
. Then there are
\lambda\in\mathcal{F}_{v}^{i}
and
\mu\in\mathcal{F}_{v}^{j}
such that
p(\lambda)=p(\mu)=e
. Because
p
is a graph homomorphism,
p(r(\lambda))=r(p(\lambda))=r(e)=p(r(\mu))
, so by unique
r
-path lifting of
p
(since
T
is LR),
\lambda=\mu
. Thus,
\mathcal{F}_{v}^{i}\cap\mathcal{F}_{v}^{j}\not=\emptyset
. Since
\{\mathcal{F}_{v}^{k}:k=1,\dots,m(v)\}
is a partition of
vF^{1}
, this implies
i=j
.

(
2
): Let
e\in p(\mathcal{F}_{v}^{i})
. Then
r(e)=p(v)=p(w)
, so by unique
r
-path lifting for
p
, there exists a unique
\eta\in F^{1}
such that
r(\eta)=w
and
p(\eta)=e
. We have
\eta\in\mathcal{F}_{w}^{j}
for some
j\in\{1,\dots,m(w)\}
. Hence,
e\in p(\mathcal{F}_{v}^{i})\cap p(\mathcal{F}_{w}^{j})
.
∎

Proposition 7.5
.

Let
T=(p,q:F\to E)
be an LR textile system with a partition
\mathcal{F}
of
F^{1}
satisfying condition (
1
) of Theorem
7.3
. If
v,w\in F^{0}
with
p(v)=p(w)
, then
m(v)=m(w)
.

Proof.

By Proposition
7.4
(
2
) and condition (
1
) of Theorem
7.3
, if
p(v)=p(w)
, then for each
i\in\{1,\dots,m(v)\}
, there exists
j_{i}\in\{1,\dots,m(w)\}
such that
p(\mathcal{F}_{v}^{i})=p(\mathcal{F}_{w}^{j_{i}})
. If
i,k\in\{1,\dots,m(v)\}
and
J:=j_{i}=j_{k}
, then
p(\mathcal{F}_{v}^{i})=p(\mathcal{F}_{w}^{J})=p(\mathcal{F}_{v}^{k})
, so
i=k
, hence
i\mapsto j_{i}
is injective. The map is surjective because for each
k\in\{1,\dots,m(w)\}
, there is
i_{k}\in\{1,\dots,m(v)\}
such that
p(\mathcal{F}_{w}^{k})=p(\mathcal{F}_{v}^{i_{k}})
(again, by combining Proposition
7.4
(
2
) and condition (
1
) of Theorem
7.3
) so
k=j_{i_{k}}
.
Hence,
m(v)=m(w)
.
∎

Remark 7.6
.

We can thus assume without loss of generality that for each
z\in E^{0}
, for all
v,w\in p^{-1}(z)
and
i=1,\dots,m(v)
,
p(\mathcal{F}_{v}^{i})=p(\mathcal{F}_{w}^{i})
, and we are justified in setting
m(z)=m(v)
.

Proposition 7.7
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
7.3
, the collection

\mathcal{E}_{z}=\{\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}:=p(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v})\mid p(v)=z,i=1,\dots,m(z)\}

is a partition of
zE^{1}
for each
z\in E^{0}
. If
\widetilde{E}
is the insplit of
E
with respect to this partition,
then the maps
\widetilde{p},\widetilde{q}:F_{JM}\to\widetilde{E}
defined,
for
v\in F^{0},

\lambda\in F^{1}
, by

\widetilde{p}(\lambda^{i})=p(\lambda)^{i},\qquad\widetilde{p}(v^{i})=p(v)^{i},

and, if
s_{F}(\lambda)=v
and
q(vF^{1})\subseteq p(\mathcal{F}_{w}^{j}),

\widetilde{q}(\lambda^{i})=q(\lambda)^{j},\qquad\widetilde{q}(v^{i})=q(v)^{j},

are graph homomorphisms, and so
\widetilde{T}:=(\widetilde{p},\widetilde{q}:F_{JM}\to\widetilde{E})
is a textile system.

Proof.

To ease the burden of notation in this proof, we abuse notation and use the same symbols
r,s
for the range and source maps in every graph which appears in the proof. We trust that the context will suffice to indicate the domain and range of each occurrence of
r,s
.

Since
p
is surjective, every
e\in zE^{1}
is in some
\mathcal{E}_{z}^{i}=p(\mathcal{F}_{v}^{i})
. Moreover, Remark
7.6
and Condition (
1
) of Theorem
7.3
indicate that
\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}\cap\mathcal{E}^{j}_{z}=\emptyset
if
i\not=j
, so
\mathcal{E}_{z}
is indeed a partition of
zE^{1}.

To see that
\widetilde{p}
is well defined, suppose that
e=p(\lambda)=p(\mu)
. Then
s(e)=s(p(\lambda))=p(s(\lambda))=p(s(\mu))
, so Remark
7.6
implies that insplitting
E
and
F
creates the same number of “copies” of
e,\lambda,
and
\mu
. It follows that

\widetilde{p}
is well-defined as claimed. To see the same for
\widetilde{q}
, we suppose
q(vF^{1})\subseteq p(\mathcal{F}_{w}^{j})\cap p(\mathcal{F}_{u}^{k})
. Since
F
is assumed to be source-free,
vF^{1}\not=\emptyset
and consequently
q(vF^{1})\neq\emptyset
. We then have
p(\mathcal{F}_{w}^{j})\cap p(\mathcal{F}_{u}^{k})\neq\emptyset
, and so Condition (
1
) of Theorem
7.3
yields
p(\mathcal{F}_{w}^{j})=p(\mathcal{F}_{u}^{k})
. Remark
7.6
implies
j=k
, and hence
\widetilde{q}
is well-defined.

Now we check that
\widetilde{p}
and
\widetilde{q}
are graph homomorphisms. First, suppose
\lambda\in\mathcal{F}_{u}^{k}
, with
s(\lambda)=v
,
q(vF^{1})\subseteq p(\mathcal{F}_{w}^{j})
, and
q(uF^{1})\subseteq p(\mathcal{F}_{\hat{u}}^{n})
. Let
e=p(\lambda)
,
f=q(\lambda)
,
z=p(u),

x=q(u)=p(\hat{u}),

y=p(v),
and
t=q(v)=p(w)
.

.
.
\scriptstyle f\in p(\mathcal{F}^{n}_{\hat{u}})
.
u
.
e
v
\scriptstyle\lambda\in\mathcal{F}^{k}_{u}
\scriptstyle x
\scriptstyle z
\scriptstyle y
\scriptstyle t
\scriptstyle q(vF^{1})\subseteq p(\mathcal{F}^{j}_{w})

Since
p,q
are graph homomorphisms,

r(e)=r(p(\lambda))=p(r(\lambda))=p(u)=z,

and similarly,
s(e)=y
,
r(f)=x
, and
s(f)=t
.
Now,

\displaystyle\widetilde{p}(s(\lambda^{i}))=\widetilde{p}(s(\lambda)^{i})=\widetilde{p}(v^{i})=p(v)^{i}=y^{i},\quad\text{ and }\quad s(\widetilde{p}(\lambda^{i}))=s(p(\lambda)^{i})=s(e^{i})=s(e)^{i}=y^{i},

so
\widetilde{p}(s(\lambda^{i}))=y^{i}=s(\widetilde{p}(\lambda^{i}))
.

Next, since
\lambda\in\mathcal{F}^{k}_{u}
, we have

\widetilde{p}(r(\lambda^{i}))=\widetilde{p}(u^{k})=p(u)^{k}=z^{k}.

On the other hand, since
e=p(\lambda)\in p(\mathcal{F}_{u}^{k})=\mathcal{E}_{z}^{k}
,

r(\widetilde{p}(\lambda^{i}))=r(p(\lambda)^{i})=r(e^{i})=z^{k}.

That is,
r(\widetilde{p}(\lambda^{i}))=z^{k}=\widetilde{p}(r(\lambda^{i}))
, and we conclude that
\widetilde{p}
is a graph homomorphism.

Now we check that
\widetilde{q}
is a graph homomorphism. First, since
\lambda\in\mathcal{F}^{k}_{u}
and
q(uF^{1})\subseteq p(\mathcal{F}^{n}_{\hat{u}}),

\widetilde{q}(r(\lambda^{i}))=\widetilde{q}(u^{k})=q(u)^{n}=x^{n},

while the facts that
s(\lambda)=v
and
q(vF^{1})\subseteq p(\mathcal{F}^{j}_{w})
imply

r(\widetilde{q}(\lambda^{i}))=r(q(\lambda)^{j})=r(f^{j})=x^{n},

since
f\in q(uF^{1})\subseteq p(\mathcal{F}_{\hat{u}}^{n})=\mathcal{E}_{x}^{n}
. So
\widetilde{q}(r(\lambda^{i}))=x^{n}=r(\widetilde{q}(\lambda^{i}))
.

Finally, since
q(vF^{1})\subseteq p(\mathcal{F}_{w}^{j})
, we have
\widetilde{q}(s(\lambda^{i}))=\widetilde{q}(v^{i})=q(v)^{j}=t^{j},

and

\displaystyle s(\widetilde{q}(\lambda^{i}))

\displaystyle=s(q(\lambda)^{j})=s(f^{j})=s(f)^{j}=t^{j}.

Thus
\widetilde{q}(s(\lambda^{i}))=t^{j}=s(\widetilde{q}(\lambda^{i}))
, and
\widetilde{q}
is also a graph homomorphism, as claimed.
∎

We have the following observation, which will be needed for the next proof.

Lemma 7.8
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
7.3
, if
u,v\in F^{0}
and
\mu\in vF^{1}
such that
q(\mu)\in p(\mathcal{F}_{u}^{i})
, then
q(vF^{1})\subseteq p(\mathcal{F}_{u}^{i})
.

Proof.

By Proposition
7.4
(
2
), there exist
w\in F^{0}
and
j\in\{1,\dots,m(w)\}
such that
q(vF^{1})\subseteq p(\mathcal{F}_{w}^{j})
. So
q(\mu)\in p(\mathcal{F}_{w}^{j})\cap p(\mathcal{F}_{u}^{i})
. Hence, by condition (
1
) in Proposition
7.4
,
p(\mathcal{F}_{w}^{j})=p(\mathcal{F}_{u}^{i})
.
∎

Proposition 7.9
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
7.3
, let
\Lambda=\Lambda_{T}
be the 2-graph of the LR textile system
T
, and for each
z\in\Lambda^{0}=E^{0}
and each
i\in\{1,\dots,m(z)\}
, set

{{\mathcal{G}_{z}^{i}=r_{F}(q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}_{z}^{i}))\sqcup\mathcal{E}_{z}^{i}}}.

Then the collection
{\mathcal{G}=\{\mathcal{G}_{z}^{i}:z\in\Lambda^{0},i=1,\dots,m(z)\}}
is a partition of
\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{1}}\sqcup\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{2}}
which satisfies the pairing condition for 2-graph insplitting.

Proof.

First we show that
\mathcal{G}
is a partition of
\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{1}}\sqcup\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{2}}(=E^{1}\sqcup F^{0})
. By Proposition
7.7
the collection
\mathcal{E}=\{\mathcal{E}_{z}^{i}:z\in E^{0}=\Lambda^{0},i=1,\dots,m(z)\}
is a partition of
E^{1}
. Hence it suffices to show that the collection
\mathcal{C}=\{F^{0}\cap\mathcal{G}_{z}^{i}=r_{F}(q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}_{z}^{i})):z\in\Lambda^{0},i=1,\dots,m(z)\}
is a partition of
F^{0}
.

Suppose
v\in F^{0}
. Since
F
is source-free,
v=r(\lambda)
for some
\lambda\in F^{1}
. Hence
q(\lambda)\in\mathcal{E}_{z}^{i}
for some
z\in E^{0},i\in\{1,\dots,m(z)\}
, and therefore
v=r(\lambda)\in r_{F}(q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}_{z}^{i}))
lies in some set in
\mathcal{C}
. To see that the sets in
\mathcal{C}
are pairwise disjoint, observe first that the hypotheses of Theorem
7.3
guarantee that
q(vF^{1})\subseteq p(\mathcal{F}^{j}_{w})
for a unique set
p(\mathcal{F}^{j}_{w}).

Thus,
fix
v\in F^{0}
, and suppose that there exist
z,x\in\Lambda^{0}=E^{0}
such that
v\in r_{F}(q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}_{z}^{i}))\cap r_{F}(q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}_{x}^{j}))
. That is, there exist
\mu,\nu\in F^{1}
such that
v=r(\mu)=r(\nu)
,
q(\mu)\in\mathcal{E}_{z}^{i}=p(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{w})
for some
w\in F^{0}
with
p(w)=z
, and
q(\nu)\in\mathcal{E}_{x}^{j}=p(\mathcal{F}^{j}_{u})
for some
u\in F^{0}
with
p(u)=x
.
As
\mu,\nu\in vF^{1}
, Lemma
7.8
forces
p(\mathcal{F}^{j}_{u})=p(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{w})
, and hence
z=x
and
i=j
. In other words,
r_{F}(q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}_{z}^{i}))\cap r(q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}_{x}^{j}))\not=\emptyset
implies
z=x
and
i=j
, so
\mathcal{C}
is indeed a partition of
F^{0}
as claimed.

For the partition
\mathcal{G}
to satisfy the pairing condition, we must have that for all
\lambda\in F^{1}
,

q(\lambda)\in\mathcal{G}_{z}^{i}
if and only if
r_{F}(\lambda)\in\mathcal{G}_{z}^{i}
. To see this,
fix
\lambda\in F^{1}
, and suppose
q(\lambda)=e\in E^{1}\cap\mathcal{G}_{z}^{i}=\mathcal{E}_{z}^{i}
. Then
r_{F}(\lambda)\in r_{F}(q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}_{z}^{i}))\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{z}^{i}
, as desired.
Now suppose
r_{F}(\lambda)=v\in F^{0}\cap\mathcal{G}_{z}^{i}=r_{F}(q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}_{z}^{i}))
. Thus there exists
\mu\in vF^{1}
such that
q(\mu)\in\mathcal{E}_{z}^{i}=p(\mathcal{F}_{u}^{i})
for some
u\in F^{0}
with
p(u)=z
. Since
q(\mu)\in p(\mathcal{F}_{u}^{i}),
Lemma
7.8

implies that
p(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{u})\supseteq q(vF^{1})\ni q(\lambda).
As
\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}=p(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{u})
, we have
q(\lambda)\in\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}\subseteq\mathcal{G}^{i}_{z}
whenever
r_{F}(\lambda)\in\mathcal{G}^{i}_{z}.
We conclude that

\mathcal{G}
satisfies the pairing condition.

∎

Thus, we can perform 2-graph insplitting on
\Lambda_{T}
with respect to the partition
\mathcal{G}
. Recall from Definitions
4.5
and
5.8
that the resulting 2-graph
\Lambda_{I}
has
\Lambda_{I}^{0}=\{z^{i}:z\in E^{0},1\leq i\leq m(z)\}
and

\Lambda_{I}^{\varepsilon_{1}}=\{e^{i}:e\in E^{1},1\leq i\leq m(s(e))\},\quad\Lambda_{I}^{\varepsilon_{2}}=\{v^{i}:v\in F^{0},1\leq i\leq m(p(v))\},

with
s_{I}(e^{i})=s(e)^{i},s_{I}(v^{i})=p(v)^{i},r_{I}(e^{i})=r(e)^{j}
if
e\in\mathcal{E}^{j}_{z}
, and
r_{I}(v^{i})=q(v)^{k}
if
v\in r_{F}(q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}^{k}_{z})),
that is, if
q(vF^{1})\subseteq p(\mathcal{F}^{k}_{u})
for some
u
with
p(u)=q(v)=z.
We have

v^{j}e^{i}\sim_{I}f^{\ell}w^{k}\iff i=k,e\in\mathcal{E}^{j}_{r(e)},w\in r_{F}(q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}^{\ell}_{s(f)})),\text{ and }ve\sim fw\in\Lambda_{T}.

That is,
v^{j}e^{i}\sim_{I}f^{\ell}w^{i}
if and only if

•

there is
\lambda\in F^{1}
with
r_{F}(\lambda)=v,s_{F}(\lambda)=w,p(\lambda)=e,q(\lambda)=f;

•

we have
q(wF^{1})\subseteq p(\mathcal{F}^{\ell}_{u})
for some
u\in F^{0}
with
p(u)=q(w)
;

•

and
\lambda\in\mathcal{F}^{j}_{v}
(so that
e=p(\lambda)\in\mathcal{E}^{j}_{r(e)}=\mathcal{E}^{j}_{p(v)}
).

In other words, each
\lambda=ve\sim_{\Lambda}fw\in F^{1}
yields commuting squares
\{\lambda^{i}:=v^{j}e^{i}\sim_{I}f^{\ell}w^{i}:i=1,\dots,m(s(e))\}
in
\Lambda_{I}
(note that
m(s(e))=m(p(w))
); the indices
j,\ell
are determined by
\lambda
and are the same for each
\lambda^{i}
.

Theorem 7.10
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
7.3
above, let
\Lambda_{I}
be the 2-graph insplitting (see Definitions
5.8
) of
\Lambda_{T}
with respect to the partition
\{\mathcal{G}_{z}^{i}:z\in\Lambda^{0},i=1,\ldots,m(z)\}
from Proposition
7.9
, and let
T_{I}=(p_{I},q_{I}:F_{I}\to E_{I})
be the textile system built from
\Lambda_{I}
as in (
11
). Then
T_{I}\cong\widetilde{T}=(\widetilde{p},\widetilde{q}:\widetilde{F}:=F_{JM}\to\widetilde{E})
, the textile system given in Proposition
7.7
.

Proof.

By (
11
) and Remark
7.6
, the LR textile system
T_{I}=(p_{I},q_{I}:F_{I}\to E_{I})
has

F_{I}^{0}=\{v^{i}:v\in F^{0},1\leq i\leq m(p(v))\},\ F_{I}^{1}=\{\lambda^{i}:\lambda\in F^{1},1\leq i\leq m(s(p(\lambda)))\},

s_{F_{1}}(\lambda^{i})=s_{F}(\lambda)^{i}
, and
\ r_{F_{I}}(\lambda^{i})=r_{F}(\lambda)^{j}\text{ if }\lambda\in\mathcal{F}^{j}_{v}.

When we compare
F_{I}
with the construction of
F_{JM}
as in Definition
5.2
, the fact that
m(p(v))=m(v)
for all
v\in F^{0}
implies that
F_{I}=F_{JM}.
Similarly, we observe that the definitions of
\widetilde{E},\widetilde{p},\widetilde{q}
in Proposition
7.7
exactly match the definitions of
E_{I},p_{I},q_{I}
from Equation (
11
). The fact that
\widetilde{E}=E_{I}
follows from the fact that the insplitting partition
\mathcal{E}
used to create
\widetilde{E}
satisfies
\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}=\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{1}}_{I}\cap\mathcal{G}^{i}_{z},
and the source and range maps in
\widetilde{E}
coincide with the 2-graph insplitting source and range maps. Moreover,
\widetilde{p}=p_{I}
and
\widetilde{q}=q_{I}
, since for any
\lambda^{i}=r_{F}(\lambda)^{k}p(\lambda)^{i}\sim_{I}q(\lambda)^{\ell}s_{F}(\lambda)^{i}\in F_{I}^{1}
and any
v^{i}\in F_{I}^{0}=\Lambda_{I}^{\varepsilon_{2}},

p_{I}(\lambda^{i})=p(\lambda)^{i},\quad p_{I}(v^{i})=s_{\Lambda_{I}}(v^{i})=p(v)^{i},\quad q_{I}(v^{i})=r_{\Lambda_{I}}(v^{i})=q(v)^{k}

if
v\in\mathcal{G}^{k}_{q(v)}
, or equivalently,
v\in r_{F}(q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}^{k}_{q(v)}).
That is,
q_{I}(v^{i})=\widetilde{q}(v^{i}).

Finally,
to compare
q_{I}(\lambda^{i})
and
\widetilde{q}(\lambda^{i})
, recall that
\lambda^{i}=r_{F}(\lambda)^{k}p(\lambda)^{i}\sim_{I}q(\lambda)^{\ell}s_{F}(\lambda)^{i}\in F_{I}^{1}
where
p(\lambda)\in\mathcal{E}^{k}_{p(r_{F}(\lambda)))}
and
s_{F}(\lambda)\in\mathcal{G}^{\ell}_{q(s_{F}(\lambda))}
. By construction, then,
q_{I}(\lambda^{i})=q(\lambda)^{\ell}
. On the other hand,
s_{F}(\lambda)\in\mathcal{G}^{\ell}_{q(s_{F}(\lambda))}
implies that
s_{F}(\lambda)\in r_{F}(q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}^{\ell}_{z}))
, so (thanks to Lemma
7.8
)
q(s_{F}(\lambda)F^{1})\subseteq p(\mathcal{F}^{\ell}_{w})
for some
w\in F^{0}
.
Consequently,
\widetilde{q}(\lambda^{i})=q(\lambda)^{\ell}=q_{I}(\lambda^{i})
as desired.

In other words,
T_{I}=\widetilde{T};
since
T_{I}
is LR, being a textile system arising from a 2-graph, it follows that
\widetilde{T}
is also LR.
∎

The above Theorem completes the proof of Theorem
7.3
.

Example 7.11
.

Consider again the LR textile system
T=(p,q:F\to E)
in Example
6.2
and its corresponding
2
-graph
\Lambda
, pictured in (
17
). Note that for each
f_{k}\in F^{0}
,
k=1,2,3
, the partition
\mathcal{F}_{f_{k}}=\{\mathcal{F}^{i}_{f_{k}}:i\leq 2\}

of Equation (
20
)

satisfies the conditions given in Theorem
7.3
.
That is, the graph
F_{A}
of Example
6.2
is the graph
F_{JM}
of Theorem
7.3
.

Observe that

q(f_{1}F^{1})=\{e_{1}\}=p(\mathcal{F}^{1}_{f_{1}})=p(\mathcal{F}^{1}_{f_{2}});\quad q(f_{2}F^{1})=\{e_{2}\}=p(\mathcal{F}^{1}_{f_{3}});\text{ and}\quad q(f_{3}F^{1})=\{e_{3}\}=p(\mathcal{F}^{2}_{f_{3}}).

As
p(f_{1})=p(f_{2})=v
and
q(f_{3})=w,
we have

\mathcal{E}^{1}_{v}=p(\mathcal{F}^{1}_{f_{1}})=\{e_{1}\}=p(\mathcal{F}^{1}_{f_{2}}),\qquad\mathcal{E}^{1}_{w}=p(\mathcal{F}^{1}_{f_{3}})=\{e_{2}\},\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{E}^{2}_{w}=\{e_{3}\}.

It follows that

the directed graph insplitting
\widetilde{E}
of
E
with this partition is precisely the directed graph
E_{\Lambda_{I}}
shown in Example
6.2
.

Thus, applying the formulas from Proposition
7.7
in this case, we obtain maps
\widetilde{p},\widetilde{q}:F_{JM}=F_{A}\to\widetilde{E}=E_{\Lambda_{I}}
which are given by

\widetilde{q}(\lambda_{1}^{1})=e_{1}^{1},\quad\widetilde{q}(\lambda_{2}^{1})=e_{2}^{1},\quad\widetilde{q}(\lambda_{3}^{1})=e_{3}^{1},\quad\widetilde{q}(\lambda_{4}^{1})=e_{3}^{2}=\widetilde{q}(\lambda_{4}^{2}),

and
\widetilde{p}(\lambda_{j}^{i})=p(\lambda_{j})^{i}
for all
i,j
.
These formulas agree with the commuting squares of
\Lambda_{I}
, as asserted by Theorem
7.10
. Indeed, note that

q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}^{1}_{v})=\{\lambda_{1}\},\quad q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}^{1}_{w})=\{\lambda_{2}\},\quad q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}^{2}_{w})=\{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}\},

so
the partition
\mathcal{G}
of Proposition
7.9
is given by

\mathcal{G}^{1}_{v}=\{f_{1},e_{1}\},\quad\mathcal{G}^{1}_{w}=\{f_{2},e_{2}\},\quad\mathcal{G}^{2}_{w}=\{f_{3},e_{3}\}.

In other words, the 2-graph insplit induced by the partition
\mathcal{F}
of Equation (
20
) is precisely the initial 2-graph insplit of Equation (
18
).

7.3.
Textile and 2-graph insplits from
E
-insplits

Although Johnson–Madden insplitting focuses on the top graph
F
of a textile system, to understand the connection between Johnson–Madden insplitting and 2-graph insplitting, it is perhaps more natural to start by insplitting the bottom graph
E
, as we now explain. This leads us to an alternate perspective to Theorem
7.3
.

Let
T=(p,q:F\to E)
be an LR textile system and let
\Lambda_{T}
denote the 2-graph associated to
T
. The following Theorem shows that by starting with a suitable partition of
E
, one can construct an LR textile system
\widetilde{T}
which yields a 2-graph
\Lambda_{I}
coinciding with the 2-graph insplitting of
\Lambda_{T}
.

Theorem 7.12
.

Let
T=(p,q:F\to E)
be an LR textile system with associated 2-graph
\Lambda
.
Assume
p
is surjective and
F
is source free.
Suppose that, for each
z\in E^{0}
,
we have a partition
\{\mathcal{E}^{1}_{z},\mathcal{E}^{2}_{z},\ldots,\mathcal{E}^{m(z)}_{z}\}
of
zE^{1}
such that
for each
u\in F^{0}
, there exists
z\in E^{0}
and
j\in\{1,2,\ldots,m(z)\}
such that
q(uF^{1})\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{j}_{z}
.

We construct a partition
\{\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}:v\in F^{0},1\leq i\leq m(p(v))\}
of
F^{1}
and a partition
\{\mathcal{G}^{i}_{z}:z\in\Lambda^{0}=E^{0},1\leq i\leq m(z)\}
of
\Lambda^{1}
as follows. For each
v\in F^{0}
and
z\in E^{0}
, define

(23)

\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}:=vF^{1}\cap p^{-1}(\mathcal{E}^{i}_{p(v)}),\;\;\;\text{ for }1\leq i\leq m(p(v)),

(24)

\mathcal{G}^{i}_{z}:=\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}\sqcup r_{F}(q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}_{z}^{i})),\;\;\;\text{ for }1\leq i\leq m(z).

Let
\widetilde{F}
denote the directed graph resulting from directed-graph insplitting
F
using the partition
\{\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}\}_{i,v}
.
Let
\widetilde{E}
denote the directed graph resulting from directed-graph insplitting
E
using the partition
\{\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}\}_{i,z}
.
Define maps
\tilde{p},\tilde{q}:\widetilde{F}\to\widetilde{E}
by

\displaystyle\tilde{p}(v^{i}):=p(v)^{i},

\displaystyle\tilde{q}(v^{i}):=q(v)^{j},

\displaystyle\tilde{p}(\lambda^{i}):=p(\lambda)^{i},

\displaystyle\tilde{q}(\lambda^{i}):=q(\lambda)^{j},

where
\lambda\in F^{1}
,
v\in F^{0}
,
s(\lambda)=v
and
j
satisfies
q(vF^{1})\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{j}_{q(v)}
. Then

(1)

The maps
\tilde{p},\tilde{q}:\widetilde{F}\to\widetilde{E}
are graph homomorphisms and
\widetilde{T}:=(\tilde{p},\tilde{q}:\widetilde{F}\to\widetilde{E})
is an LR textile system.

(2)

The collection
\{\mathcal{G}^{i}_{z}:=\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}\sqcup r(q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}_{z}^{i})):z\in E^{0},1\leq i\leq m(z)\}
satisfies the pairing condition.

(3)

The 2-graph associated to
\widetilde{T}
(denoted by
\widetilde{\Lambda}
) is identical to the 2-graph
{\Lambda}_{I}
resulting from insplitting
\Lambda
using the partition
\{\mathcal{G}^{j}_{z}\}_{j,z}.

Proof.

We begin by observing that if
q(uF^{1})\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{j}_{z}
, then
z=q(u)
and
j
is unique, since
q
is a graph homomorphism and
\{\mathcal{E}^{j}_{z}\}_{j}
is a partition of
zE^{1}
. Thus,
\tilde{q}
is well defined.

Next, we show that
\{\mathcal{F}_{v}^{i}:v\in F^{0},1\leq i\leq m(p(v))\}
is a well-defined partition of
F^{1}
. First, note that if
v\in F^{0}
and
p(v)=z
, then

(25)

vF^{1}\cap p^{-1}(\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z})\neq\emptyset\text{ for all }i\in\{1,2,\ldots,m(z)\}.

This is because there exists some
e\in\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}
(partition sets are nonempty by convention), and since
p(v)=z=r(e)
, unique
r
-path lifting of
p
implies that there exists a unique
\lambda\in F^{1}
such that
r_{F}(\lambda)=v
and
p(\lambda)=e
. Hence,
\lambda\in vF^{1}\cap p^{-1}(\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z})
.
It follows that
\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}\neq\emptyset
for all
v\in F^{0}
and all
1\leq i\leq m(p(v))
.
Consequently,
\{\mathcal{F}_{v}^{i}:v\in F^{0},1\leq i\leq m(p(v))\}
is a well-defined partition of
F^{1}
:

\displaystyle\bigcup_{i}\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}=vF^{1}\cap p^{-1}(\bigcup_{i}\mathcal{E}^{i}_{p(v)})=vF^{1}\cap p^{-1}(p(v)E^{1})=vF^{1},

and

\mathcal{F}^{k}_{u}\cap\mathcal{F}^{l}_{v}\neq\emptyset\implies\left(v=u\text{ and }\mathcal{E}^{k}_{p(u)}\cap\mathcal{E}^{l}_{p(v)}\neq\emptyset\right)\implies v=u\text{ and }k=l.

In fact, the partition
\{\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}\}_{i,v}
satisfies Hypotheses (1) and (2) of Theorem
7.3
. To see this, first observe that the sets
\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}
and
\mathcal{F}^{i}_{w}
of the current Theorem satisfy
\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}=p(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v})
for any
v
with
p(v)=z
. (The inclusion
\supseteq
is immediate; to see equality, choose
e\in\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}
and
v\in F^{0}
with
p(v)=z
. Then
r
-path lifting yields
f\in F^{1}
with
r(f)=v
and
p(f)=e
, that is,
f\in p^{-1}(\mathcal{E}^{i}_{p(v)})\cap vF^{1}=\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}
and
e=p(f)
.) Thus, (1) holds since
\{\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}\}_{i,z}
is a partition of
E^{1}
, and (2) holds by our hypothesis on the partition
\{\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}\}_{i,z}
.

Consequently, the graph
\widetilde{E}
of the current theorem is the same as the graph
\widetilde{E}
of Theorem
7.3
, and our
\widetilde{F}
is the graph
F_{JM}
of Theorem
7.3
. Moreover, the maps
\tilde{p},\tilde{q}:\widetilde{F}\to\widetilde{E}
defined in the statement of the current theorem are precisely the maps discussed in Proposition
7.7
. Hence, that proposition establishes that
\tilde{p},\tilde{q}:\widetilde{F}\to\widetilde{E}
are well-defined graph homomorphisms. The fact that
\widetilde{T}:=(\tilde{p},\tilde{q}:\widetilde{F}\to\widetilde{E})
is an LR textile system follows from the identification
\widetilde{T}=T_{I}
of Theorem
7.10
.

Finally, observe that the sets
\mathcal{G}^{i}_{z}
of the current theorem are precisely the same as those of Proposition
7.9
, so that proposition yields statement (2) of the theorem; and the proof of Theorem
7.10
yields statement (3).
∎

7.4.
Uniting the approaches

As mentioned at the beginning of this Section, the relationships between 2-graph insplitting and Johnson–Madden insplitting established in Theorems
7.1
,
7.3
, and
7.12
are compatible. That is, the coherent choice of notation in Theorems
7.1
,
7.3
, and
7.12
was neither accidental nor an abuse of notation, because all the constructions in these results are equivalent.
To obtain a clean statement of the equivalences, we assume throughout this section that the top graph
F
is source-free and the graph homomorphism
p
is surjective, as done in Theorem
7.3
.

Remark 7.13
.

While it is sufficient to assume that
p
is surjective and
F
is source-free, we note that the standard dynamical assumption of an essential system (essential textile system
T
or equivalently essential 2-graph
\Lambda_{T}
) also implies all the results in this section.

We begin by showing that the setup in Theorem
7.1
leads to the results in Theorem
7.3
.

Theorem 7.14

(Theorem
7.1

\longrightarrow
Theorem
7.3
)
.

Let
T=(p,q:F\to E)
be an LR textile system and let
\Lambda
be its associated 2-graph. Further, assume that
p
is surjective and
F
is source free.
Let
\{\mathcal{G}^{i}_{z}:z\in E^{0},1\leq i\leq m(z)\}
be a 2-graph insplitting partition of
\Lambda
. Then,

(1)

The partition
\{\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}:v\in F^{0},1\leq i\leq m(v)\}
of
F^{1}
defined in Theorem
7.1
as

(26)

\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}:=\{\lambda\in F^{1}:r(\lambda)=v,\;\;p(\lambda)\in\mathcal{G}^{i}_{r(p(\lambda))=p(v)}\}

satisfies the hypotheses of Theorem
7.3
, namely:

(a)

If
v,w\in F^{0}
such that
p(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v})\cap p(\mathcal{F}^{j}_{w})\neq\emptyset
, then
p(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v})=p(\mathcal{F}^{j}_{w})
; and

(b)

For each
v\in F^{0}
, there exists
w\in F^{0}
and
1\leq j\leq m(w)
such that
q(vF^{1})\subseteq p(\mathcal{F}^{j}_{w})
.

(2)

The partition
\{\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}:z\in E^{0},1\leq i\leq m(z)\}
of
E^{1}
defined in Theorem
7.1
by

(27)

\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}:=\mathcal{G}^{i}_{z}\cap zE^{1}

satisfies
\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}=p(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v})
for any
v\in F^{0}
such that
z=p(v)
, and hence agrees with the corresponding partition of
E^{1}
in Theorem
7.3
.

(3)

The original 2-graph partition
\{\mathcal{G}^{i}_{z}:z\in E^{0},1\leq i\leq m(z)\}
satisfies

(28)

\mathcal{G}^{i}_{z}=\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}\sqcup r_{F}(q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}))

for all
v
such that
z=p(v)\in E^{0}
and for all
1\leq i\leq m(v)
.
In particular, the 2-graph insplitting partition constructed in Theorem
7.3
agrees with the original partition
\{\mathcal{G}^{i}_{z}:z\in E^{0},1\leq i\leq m(z)\}
.

(4)

The constructions of the textile system
\widetilde{T}=(\widetilde{p},\widetilde{q}:\widetilde{F}\to\widetilde{E})
in Theorems
7.1
and
7.3
coincide.

Proof.

We first observe that for any
v\in F^{0}
and
1\leq i\leq m(p(v)),

(29)

p(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v})=\mathcal{G}^{i}_{p(v)}\cap E^{1}.

The containment
\subseteq
follows from the definition, and the reverse containment follows from
r
-path lifting for
p
. It immediately follows that (2) holds.
Furthermore,
p(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v})=p(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{w})

whenever
p(v)=p(w).

To see (1a), recall from Theorem
7.1
that
\{\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}\}_{i,z}
is a partition of
E^{1}
. Thus, (1a) follows from Equation (
29
).

For (1b), suppose
\lambda\in vF^{1}
has
q(\lambda)\in p(\mathcal{F}^{j}_{w})=\mathcal{E}^{j}_{p(w)}\subseteq\mathcal{G}^{j}_{p(w)}
. (For each
v
, such a
\lambda
exists since
F
is source free.) As
\{\mathcal{G}^{j}_{z}\}_{j,z}
satisfies the pairing condition, we have
v\in\mathcal{G}^{j}_{z}
as well. Thus, the pairing condition, together with the fact that
p(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{u})=p(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{w})
whenever
p(u)=p(w)
, implies that for any
\mu\in vF^{1}
,
q(\mu)\in\mathcal{G}^{j}_{z}\cap E^{1}=p(\mathcal{F}^{j}_{w}).
That is, (1b) holds.

For (3), Equation (
27
) implies that
\mathcal{G}^{i}_{z}\cap E^{1}=\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}
. To see that
\mathcal{G}^{i}_{z}\cap F^{0}=r_{F}(q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}))
, we use the fact that
F
is source free, the pairing condition, and the definition of
r_{\Lambda}
. From these, we conclude that
w\in F^{0}\cap\mathcal{G}^{i}_{z}
if and only if
q(w)=r_{\Lambda}(w)=z
and there is
\lambda\in F^{1}
with
r_{F}(\lambda)=w
and
q(\lambda)\in\mathcal{G}^{i}_{z}\cap E^{1}=\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}.
That is,
w\in F^{0}\cap\mathcal{G}^{i}_{z}
if and only if
w\in r_{F}(q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}))
, so (3) holds.

As we have now proved that the partitions
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{F}
constructed in Theorem
7.1
have the same properties as the partitions of Theorem
7.3
, and the formulas for
\tilde{p},\tilde{q}
are the same in both Theorems, (4) holds.

∎

We next show that starting from the setting in Theorem
7.3
, one can obtain the results in Theorem
7.12
.

Theorem 7.15

(Theorem
7.3

\longrightarrow
Theorem
7.12
)
.

Let
T=(p,q:F\to E)
be an LR textile system with associated 2-graph
\Lambda
. Further, assume that
p
is surjective and
F
is source free.
Let
\{\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}:v\in F^{0},1\leq i\leq m(v)\}
be a partition of
F^{1}
as in Theorem
7.3
, namely:

(a)

If
v,w\in F^{0}
such that
p(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v})\cap p(\mathcal{F}^{j}_{w})\neq\emptyset
, then
p(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v})=p(\mathcal{F}^{j}_{w})
; and

(b)

For each
v\in F^{0}
, there exists
w\in F^{0}
and
1\leq j\leq m(w)
such that
q(vF^{1})\subseteq p(\mathcal{F}^{j}_{w})
.

Then,

(1)

The partition
\{\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}:z\in E^{0},1\leq i\leq m(v)\}
of
E^{1}

defined in Theorem
7.3
as
\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}:=p(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v})
, where
p(v)=z
,
satisfies the hypothesis of Theorem
7.12
, that is,

For each
u\in F^{0}
, there is a unique
j\in\{1,2,\ldots,m(q(u))\}
such that
q(uF^{1})\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{j}_{q(u)}
.

(2)

The partition
\{\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}:v\in F^{0},1\leq i\leq m(v)\}
of
F^{1}
constructed from
\{\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}\}_{i,z}
in Theorem
7.12
agrees with the original partition
\{\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}\}
.

Consequently, the 2-graph insplitting partitions and the textile systems
\widetilde{T}
constructed in the two Theorems coincide.

Proof.

Let
\{\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}:v\in F^{0},1\leq i\leq m(v)\}
be a partition of
F^{1}
as in Theorem
7.3
.

(1)

Recall from Proposition
7.7
that

\{\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}:=p(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v})\}_{i,z}
, where
z=p(v)
, is a well-defined partition of
E^{1}
.
Fix
u\in F^{0}
. Then by hypothesis (b), there exists
w\in F^{0}
and
1\leq j\leq m(w)
such that
q(uF^{1})\subseteq p(\mathcal{F}^{j}_{w})=\mathcal{E}^{j}_{p(w)}
.
As
\mathcal{E}^{j}_{p(w)}\subseteq p(w)E^{1}
and
r_{E}(q(uF^{1}))=q(r_{F}(uF^{1}))=q(u)
, we must have
q(u)=p(w)
. As
\mathcal{E}
is a partition and hence
\mathcal{E}^{k}_{q(u)}\cap\mathcal{E}^{j}_{q(u)}\neq\emptyset
implies
k=j
, (1) holds.

(2)

It is sufficient to prove that for all
v\in F^{0}
and
1\leq i\leq m(v)
,

\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}=vF^{1}\cap p^{-1}(p(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v})).

Clearly,
\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}\subseteq vF^{1}\cap p^{-1}(p(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}))
. To prove the opposite containment, suppose that
f\in vF^{1}\cap p^{-1}(p(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}))

but
f\in\mathcal{F}^{k}_{v}
for some
k
. Then
p(f)=p(\lambda)
for some
\lambda\in\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}
, i.e.,
p(f)\in p(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v})\cap p(\mathcal{F}^{k}_{v})
, so by hypothesis (a),
p(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v})=p(\mathcal{F}^{k}_{v})
. Proposition
7.4
now tells us that
i=k
, as desired.

The 2-graph insplitting partitions constructed in both Theorems are the same, by definition:

(30)

\{\mathcal{G}^{i}_{z}:=\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}\sqcup r_{F}(q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}_{z}^{i})):z\in E^{0},1\leq i\leq m(z)\}.

Finally,
as we have already observed that the partitions used to construct
\widetilde{F}
and
\widetilde{E}
in Theorems
7.3
and
7.12
coincide, the graphs
\widetilde{F},\widetilde{E}
resulting from the two Theorems are the same. Moreover, the two Theorems use the same definitions of
\tilde{p},\tilde{q}
, thus, the two textile systems coincide.∎

Lastly, we show that starting from the setting of Theorem
7.12
, we recover the results in Theorem
7.1
.

Theorem 7.16

(Theorem
7.12

\longrightarrow
Theorem
7.1
)
.

Let
T=(p,q:F\to E)
be an LR textile system with associated 2-graph
\Lambda
. Further, assume that
p
is surjective and
F
is source free.
Let
\{\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}:z\in E^{0},1\leq i\leq m(z)\}
be a partition of
E^{1}
as in Theorem
7.12
, so that for each
u\in F^{0}
, there exist
z=q(u)\in E^{0}
and a unique
j\in\{1,2,\ldots,m(z)\}
such that
q(uF^{1})\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{j}_{z}
.

Define partitions of
F^{1}
and
\Lambda^{1}
as in Theorem
7.12
, namely: For each
v\in F^{0}
and
z\in E^{0}
, let

(31)

\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}:=vF^{1}\cap p^{-1}(\mathcal{E}^{i}_{p(v)}),\;\;\;\text{ for }1\leq i\leq m(p(v)),

(32)

\mathcal{G}^{i}_{z}:=\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}\sqcup r_{F}(q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}_{z}^{i})),\;\;\;\text{ for }1\leq i\leq m(z).

Then,

(1)

The partition of
E^{1}
constructed in Theorem
7.1
from the 2-graph partition (
32
) agrees with the original partition
\{\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}\}
, that is

\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}=\mathcal{G}^{i}_{z}\cap zE^{1}.

(2)

The partition of
F^{1}
constructed in Theorem
7.1
from
\{\mathcal{G}^{i}_{z}\}_{i,z}
agrees with the partition of Equation (
31
), that is

\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}=\{\lambda\in vF^{1}:p(\lambda)\in\mathcal{G}^{i}_{p(v)}\}.

(3)

The constructions of the textile system
\widetilde{T}=(\tilde{p},\tilde{q}:\widetilde{F}\to\widetilde{E})
in Theorems
7.1
and
7.12
coincide.

Proof.

Following the fact that
\mathcal{G}^{i}_{z}\cap zE^{1}=\bigg(\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}\sqcup r(q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}_{z}^{i}))\bigg)\cap zE^{1}=\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}\cap zE^{1}=\mathcal{E}^{i}_{z}
, we obtain (1).
For (2), we have
f\in\mathcal{F}^{i}_{v}
if and only if
f\in\{\lambda\in vF^{1}:p(\lambda)\in\mathcal{E}^{i}_{p(v)}\}
which is equivalent to
f\in\{\lambda\in vF^{1}:p(\lambda)\in\mathcal{G}^{i}_{p(v)}\}
as
p(f)\in E^{1}
but
p(f)\notin r_{F}(q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}_{z}^{i}))
.

For (3), it is sufficient to show that the maps
\tilde{q}
defined in Theorem
7.1
and Theorem
7.12
are the same. To this end, we note that for
v\in F^{0}
,

\displaystyle v\in\mathcal{G}^{m}_{q(v)}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle v\in r_{F}(q^{-1}(\mathcal{E}^{m}_{q(v)}))\iff\exists\lambda\in vF^{1}\text{ such that }q(\lambda)\in\mathcal{E}^{m}_{q(v)}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle q(vF^{1})\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{m}_{q(v)},

where the last equivalence follows from the hypothesis.
So, the definition of
\tilde{q}
in Theorem
7.1
and Theorem
7.12
coincide, and hence the textile systems are the same.
∎

Appendix A
C^{*}
algebraic results

In this appendix, we discuss the
C^{*}
-algebraic implications of our work. First, we show in Theorem
A.3
that the
C^{*}
-algebra of a 2-graph is unchanged under a 2-graph insplitting; this result was established in
[
EFG+22
, Theorem 3.9]
for the one-vertex-at-a-time version of 2-graph insplitting discussed in that paper. We also show that this isomorphism is gauge-invariant and diagonal-preserving. Thus,
[
CR21
, Theorem 3.2]
shows that 2-graph insplitting yields an eventual 1-sided conjugacy. (In fact, as we have expressed 2-graph insplitting as a composition of conjugacy-preserving moves on textile systems in Theorem
6.1
, we have already proved the stronger result that 2-graph insplitting is a 1-sided conjugacy.) We then define outsplitting for 2-graphs, as introduced in
[
Lis24
]
, and explain why this paper has focused on linking the textile-system and 2-graph definitions of insplitting rather than outsplitting.

To simplify our calculations we will use an edge-based definition of
2
-graph algebras which has seen some light in the literature, see
[
EFG+22
, Definition 2.1.1]
,
[
RSY04
, Theorem C.1]
,
[
KPS12
, Definition 7.4]
and alluded to in
[
KP00
, Remarks 1.6]
.

Definition A.1

(Edge definition of a
2
-graph
C^{*}
-algebra)
.

Let
\Lambda
be a row-finite
2
-graph. An
edge Cuntz–Krieger

\Lambda
-family
in a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
is a function
s:\Lambda^{0}\cup\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{1}}\cup\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{2}}\to\text{PIsom}(A)
, denoted
x\mapsto s_{x}
, which assigns a partial isometry
s_{x}
to each
x\in\Lambda^{0}\cup\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{1}}\cup\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{2}}
such that

(ECK1)

\{s_{v}:v\in\Lambda^{0}\}
is a collection of mutually orthogonal projections;

(ECK2)

s_{e}s_{f}=s_{f^{\prime}}s_{e^{\prime}}
whenever
e,e^{\prime}\in\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{1}}
,
f,f^{\prime}\in\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{2}}
and
ef\sim f^{\prime}e^{\prime}
;

(ECK3)

s^{*}_{f}s_{f}=s_{s(f)}
for all
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}}
; and

(ECK4)

s_{v}=\sum_{f\in v\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{i}}}s_{f}s_{f}^{*}
for all
v\in\Lambda^{0}
and
i=1,2
.

The
C^{*}
-algebra
C^{*}(\Lambda)
is the universal
C^{*}
-algebra generated by an edge Cuntz–Krieger
\Lambda
-family, and we often write
\{t_{\lambda}:\lambda\in\Lambda^{0}\cup\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{1}}\cup\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{2}}\}
for the generators of the universal
C^{*}
-algebra
C^{*}(\Lambda)
. That is, for any edge Cuntz–Krieger family
s:\Lambda^{0}\cup\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{1}}\cup\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{2}}\to A
, there is a unique surjective
*
-homomorphism
\pi_{s}
from
C^{*}(\Lambda)
onto the
C^{*}
-algebra generated by the image of
s
, which satisfies
\pi_{s}(t_{\lambda})=s_{\lambda}
for all generators
t_{\lambda}
.
2
2

2

The existence of
C^{*}(\Lambda)
can be proved by following for instance
[
Rae05
, Proposition 1.21]
.

For general elements
\lambda\in\Lambda
, if
e_{1}\cdots e_{n}
is a representative of
\Lambda
with each
e_{j}\in\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{i}}
for some
i=1,2
, we define
t_{\lambda}=t_{e_{1}}\cdots t_{e_{n}}.
Condition (ECK2) guarantees that
t_{\lambda}
is independent of the choice of representative of
\lambda
.

Because of the universal property of
C^{*}(\Lambda)
, there is a canonical action
\gamma
of
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
on
C^{*}(\Lambda)
, called the
gauge action
, which satisfies

\gamma_{z}(t_{e})=z^{d(e)}t_{e}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\gamma_{z}(t_{v})=t_{v}

for all
z\in{\mathbb{T}}^{2},e\in\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{1}}\sqcup\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{2}}
and
v\in\Lambda^{0}
. A standard
\varepsilon/3
argument shows that this action is strongly continuous.

Remarks A.2

(Switching between edge-based and path-based models for
C^{*}(\Lambda)
)
.

One can extract from
[
KPS15
,
KPS12
]
a proof that Definition
A.1
and the original definition
[
KP00
, Definitions 1.5]
of
C^{*}(\Lambda)
agree. To be precise,
Definition
A.1
corresponds to the definition of Cuntz–Krieger
\iota
-family given in
[
KPS12
, Definition 7.4]
, where
\iota
denotes the trivial cubical cocycle
\iota\in Z^{2}(\Lambda,\mathbb{T})
, so the universal
C^{*}
-algebra of Definition
A.1
agrees with the
C^{*}
-algebra
C^{*}_{\iota}(\Lambda)
of
[
KPS12
, Definition 7.5]
. Then
[
KPS15
, Theorem 3.16]
identifies
\iota
with the trivial categorical cocycle
c_{\iota}\in\underline{Z}^{2}(\Lambda,\mathbb{T})
. Next
[
KPS15
, Theorem 5.3]
gives a gauge-equivariant isomorphism
\phi:C_{\iota}^{*}(\Lambda)\to C^{*}(\Lambda,c_{\iota})
. Finally, one checks that
C^{*}(\Lambda,c_{\iota})
defines the same object as the original
C^{*}(\Lambda)
from
[
KP00
, Definitions 1.5]
, as both are universal
C^{*}
-algebras with the same generators and relations.

In order to link our work with that of Carlsen and Rout
[
CR21
]
, we recall that the
diagonal subalgebra

\mathcal{D}(\Lambda)
of
C^{*}(\Lambda)
is the Abelian subalgebra densely spanned by
\{t_{\lambda}t_{\lambda}^{*}:\lambda\in\Lambda\}.

Theorem A.3

(
2
-graph insplitting induces gauge-invariant isomorphism)
.

Let
\Lambda
be an essential row-finite 2-graph with associated 1-skeleton
(G_{\Lambda},c_{\Lambda})
. Let
\mathcal{E}
be an insplitting partition of
(G_{\Lambda},c_{\Lambda})
satisfying Definition
5.8
. Let
\Lambda_{I}
be the 2-graph resulting from insplitting
\Lambda
with this partition. Then there is a gauge-equivariant diagonal-preserving isomorphism
\pi_{S}:C^{*}(\Lambda)\to C^{*}(\Lambda_{I})
.

Proof.

Let
\{s_{v},s_{f}:v\in\Lambda^{0},f\in\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{1}}\sqcup\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{2}}\}
be an edge Cuntz-Krieger
\Lambda
-family generating
C^{*}(\Lambda)

and
\{t_{v^{j}},t_{f^{j}}:v^{j}\in\Lambda_{I}^{0},f^{j}\in\Lambda_{I}^{\varepsilon_{1}}\sqcup\Lambda_{I}^{\varepsilon_{2}}\}
be an edge Cuntz-Krieger
\Lambda_{I}
-family generating
C^{*}(\Lambda_{I})
. For
v\in\Lambda^{0}
and
f\in\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{1}}\sqcup\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{2}}
set

S_{v}=\sum_{1\leq i\leq m(v)}t_{v^{i}}\ \text{ and }\ S_{f}=\sum_{1\leq j\leq m(s(f))}t_{f^{j}}.

We claim that the family
\{S_{v},S_{f}:v\in\Lambda^{0},f\in\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{1}}\sqcup\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{2}}\}
is an edge Cuntz-Krieger
\Lambda
-family in
C^{*}(\Lambda_{I})
. To do this we first observe that each
S_{f}
is a partial isometry since it is a finite sum of partial isometries with different source projections (cf.
[
MPR14
, Lemma 2.1]
).

Next we check that this family
\{S_{f},S_{v}\}
satisfies the relations (ECK1)–(ECK4).

We begin with (ECK1). The
S_{v}
’s are non-zero mutually orthogonal projections since they are sums of projections satisfying the same properties. For (ECK3) let
f\in\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{i}}
,
i=1,2
. Since the generators
t_{v^{j}},t_{f^{j}}
of
C^{*}(\Lambda_{I})
satisfy (ECK1) and (ECK3),

\displaystyle S_{f}^{*}S_{f}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{1\leq j\leq m(s(f))}t_{f^{j}}\right)^{*}\left(\sum_{1\leq j\leq m(s(f))}t_{f^{j}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq j\leq m(s(f))}t_{f^{j}}^{*}t_{f^{j}}\text{ since }s(f^{i})=s(f)^{i}\neq s(f)^{j}=s(f^{j})\text{ if}\ i\neq j

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq j\leq m(s(f))}t_{s(f)^{j}}=S_{s(f)}\text{ by definition.}

For (ECK2) let
g,g^{\prime}\in\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{1}}
and
h,h^{\prime}\in\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{2}}
be such that
gh\sim h^{\prime}g^{\prime}
in
\Lambda
. Suppose that
h\in\mathcal{E}_{s(g)}^{\ell}
and
g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}_{s(h^{\prime})}^{m}
. Then the fact that
T:=\{t_{x}:x\in\Lambda_{I}^{0}\cup\Lambda_{I}^{\varepsilon_{1}}\cup\Lambda_{I}^{\varepsilon_{2}}\}
is a family of partial isometries satisfying (ECK1) allows us to conclude that

t_{g^{j}}t_{h^{k}}=t_{g^{j}}t_{s(g)^{j}}t_{r(h)^{\ell}}t_{h^{k}}

is zero unless
j=\ell
. Moreover, the definition of
\sim_{I}
, together with the fact that
T
satisfies (ECK2), implies that
t_{g^{\ell}}t_{h^{k}}=t_{(h^{\prime})^{m}}t_{(g^{\prime})^{k}}.
Since
gh\sim h^{\prime}g^{\prime}
implies in particular that
s(h)=s(g^{\prime})
,

\displaystyle S_{g}S_{h}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{1\leq j\leq m(s(g))}t_{g^{j}}\right)\left(\sum_{1\leq k\leq m(s(h))}t_{h^{k}}\right)=\sum_{1\leq k\leq m(s(h))}t_{g^{\ell}}t_{h^{k}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq k\leq m(s(g^{\prime}))}t_{(h^{\prime})^{m}}t_{(g^{\prime})^{k}}=\sum_{1\leq j^{\prime}\leq m(s(h^{\prime}))}t_{(h^{\prime})^{j^{\prime}}}\sum_{1\leq k\leq m(s(g^{\prime}))}t_{(g^{\prime})^{k}}

\displaystyle=S_{h^{\prime}}S_{g^{\prime}}.

For (ECK4), recall that every
g\in\Lambda_{I}^{\varepsilon_{i}}
is of the form
g=f^{k}
for some
f\in\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{i}}
, and that
r_{\Lambda_{I}}(f^{k})=r_{\Lambda}(f)^{j}
if
f\in\mathcal{E}^{j}_{r(f)}
. Therefore, for any
v\in\Lambda^{0}
and
i=1,2
we can write

v\Lambda_{I}^{\varepsilon_{i}}=\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq m(v)}\bigcup_{f\in\mathcal{E}^{j}_{v}}\{f^{k}:1\leq k\leq m(s_{\Lambda}(f))\}.

Moreover, since
t_{f^{k}}=t_{f^{k}}t_{s(f)^{k}}
by (ECK3), we have
t_{f^{k}}t_{f^{\ell}}^{*}=\delta_{\ell,k}t_{f^{k}}t_{f^{\ell}}^{*}
by (ECK1).
It follows that

\displaystyle S_{v}=\sum_{1\leq j\leq m(v)}t_{v^{j}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq j\leq m(v)}\sum_{f\in\mathcal{E}_{v}^{j}}\sum_{1\leq k\leq m(s(f))}t_{f^{k}}t_{f^{k}}^{*}\text{ by (ECK4) in $C^{*}(\Lambda_{I})$}

\displaystyle=\sum_{f\in v\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{i}}}\sum_{1\leq k\leq m(s(f))}t_{f^{k}}t_{f^{k}}^{*}=\sum_{f\in v\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{i}}}\sum_{1\leq k,\ell\leq m(s(f))}t_{f^{k}}t_{f^{\ell}}^{*}

\displaystyle=\sum_{f\in v\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{i}}}S_{f}S_{f}^{*},

so
\{S_{v},S_{f}\}
satisfies (ECK4). By the universal property of
C^{*}(\Lambda)
there is a
*
-homomorphism
\pi_{S}:C^{*}(\Lambda)\to C^{*}(\Lambda_{I})
taking
s_{x}
to
S_{x}
for all
x\in\Lambda^{0}\cup\big(\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{1}}\sqcup\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{2}}\big)
.
Moreover, the definition of
S_{x}
ensures that
\pi_{S}
is gauge-equivariant.

We claim that
\pi_{S}
is onto. To do this we show that the generators of
C^{*}(\Lambda_{I})
lie in the image
C^{*}(\{S_{x}:x\in\Lambda^{0}\cup(\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{1}}\sqcup\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{2}})\})
of
\pi_{S}
.

Fix
v^{j}
, where
1\leq j\leq m(v)
. Recall that, for any
i=1,2
,

v^{j}\Lambda_{I}^{\varepsilon_{i}}=\{f^{k}:d(f)=\varepsilon_{i},f\in\mathcal{E}^{j}_{v},1\leq k\leq m(s(f))\}.

Using the fact that the generators of
C^{*}(\Lambda_{I})
satisfy (ECK4), we conclude that for each
i=1,2,

S_{v}\left(\sum_{f\in\mathcal{E}^{j}_{v}\cap\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{i}}}S_{f}S_{f}^{*}\right)=\left(\sum_{1\leq k\leq m(v)}t_{v^{k}}\right)\left(\sum_{f\in\mathcal{E}^{j}_{v}\cap\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{i}}}\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq m(s(f))}t_{f^{\ell}}t_{f^{\ell}}^{*}\right)=t_{v^{j}}\sum_{g\in v^{j}\Lambda_{I}^{\varepsilon_{i}}}t_{g}t_{g}^{*}=t_{v^{j}},

so each generator
t_{v^{j}}
of
C^{*}(\Lambda_{I})
lies in the image of
\pi_{S}
.

Applying (ECK1) now implies that, for any
1\leq j\leq m(s(f))
,

t_{f^{j}}=t_{f^{j}}t_{s(f)^{j}}=\left(\sum_{1\leq k\leq m(s(f))}t_{f^{k}}\right)t_{s(f)^{j}}=S_{f}t_{s(f)^{j}}

also lies in the image of
\pi_{S}
. We conclude that
\pi_{S}:C^{*}(\Lambda)\to C^{*}(\Lambda_{I})
is onto.

To see that
\pi_{S}
is injective, we invoke the gauge-invariant uniqueness theorem
[
KP00
, Theorem 3.4]
. Since
\pi_{S}
is gauge-equivariant, it is injective because
S_{v}
is nonzero for any
v\in\Lambda^{0}
, thanks to the universal property of
C^{*}(\Lambda_{I})
. That is,
\pi_{S}
is a
*
-isomorphism.

Finally, we check that
\pi_{S}(\mathcal{D}(\Lambda))=\mathcal{D}(\Lambda_{I}).
Given
\lambda\in\Lambda
, choose a representative
e_{1}\cdots e_{n}
of
\Lambda
with each
e_{j}\in\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{i}}
for some
i=1,2
. As
t_{e_{j}^{k}}t_{e_{j+1}^{m}}=0
unless
e_{j+1}\in\mathcal{E}_{s(e_{j})}^{k},

S_{e_{1}}\cdots S_{e_{n}}=\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq m(s(e_{n}))}t_{e_{1}^{m_{1}}}\cdots t_{e_{n-1}^{m_{n-1}}}t_{e_{n}^{\ell}},

where
e_{j}\in\mathcal{E}^{m_{j-1}}_{r(e_{j})}
for all
2\leq j\leq n.
By (ECK1) and (ECK3),
t_{e_{n}^{\ell}}t_{e_{n}^{k}}^{*}=\delta_{\ell,k}t_{e_{n}^{\ell}}t_{e_{n}^{\ell}}^{*}
. Thus,

S_{\lambda}S_{\lambda}^{*}=(S_{e_{1}}\cdots S_{e_{n}})(S_{e_{1}}\cdots S_{e_{n}})^{*}=\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq m(s(e_{n}))}t_{e_{1}^{m_{1}}}\cdots t_{e_{n-1}^{m_{n-1}}}t_{e_{n}^{\ell}}(t_{e_{1}^{m_{1}}}\cdots t_{e_{n-1}^{m_{n-1}}}t_{e_{n}^{\ell}})^{*}

lies in
\mathcal{D}(\Lambda_{I})
, being a finite sum of elements of the form
t_{\lambda}t_{\lambda}^{*}.
As every
*
-isomorphism is norm-preserving, we conclude that
\pi_{S}(\mathcal{D}(\Lambda))\subseteq\mathcal{D}(\Lambda_{I}).

To show that
\pi_{S}(\mathcal{D}(\Lambda))=\mathcal{D}(\Lambda_{I})
, since
\pi_{S}
is norm-preserving it suffices to show that
t_{\lambda}t_{\lambda}^{*}\in\pi_{S}(\mathcal{D}(\Lambda))
for all
\lambda\in\Lambda_{I}
. So, fix
\lambda=e_{1}^{j_{1}}e_{2}^{j_{2}}\cdots e_{n}^{j_{n}}\in\Lambda_{I}
, and observe that since
s(e_{n}^{j_{n}})=s(e_{n})^{j_{n}},

s(\lambda)\Lambda_{I}^{\varepsilon_{i}}=\{e^{\ell}:e\in\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{i}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{s(e_{n})}^{j_{n}},\ 1\leq\ell\leq m(s(e))\}.

Therefore, by (ECK4),

t_{\lambda}=\sum_{e\in\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{i}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{s(e_{n})}^{j_{n}}}\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq m(s(e))}t_{\lambda}t_{e^{\ell}}t_{e^{\ell}}^{*}=\sum_{e\in\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{i}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{s(e_{n})}^{j_{n}}}t_{\lambda}S_{e}S_{e}^{*}.

As above, recall that for all
1\leq i\leq n-1
, there is a unique
1\leq j_{i}\leq m(s(e_{i}))
so that
t_{e_{1}}^{j_{1}}\cdots t_{e_{n}}^{j_{n}}\not=0
[namely,
j_{i}=m
iff
e_{i+1}\in\mathcal{E}^{m}_{s(e_{i})}
]. Consequently, using the fact that
s(e_{n}^{k})=s(e_{n})^{k},

\sum_{e\in\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{i}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{s(e_{n})}^{j_{n}}}S_{e_{1}}\cdots S_{e_{n}}S_{e}(S_{e_{1}}\cdots S_{e_{n}}S_{e})^{*}=\sum_{e\in\Lambda^{\varepsilon_{i}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{s(e_{n})}^{j_{n}}}t_{\lambda}S_{e}S_{e}^{*}t_{\lambda}^{*}=t_{\lambda}t_{\lambda}^{*}\in\pi_{S}(\mathcal{D}(\Lambda)).

We conclude that
\pi_{S}
is diagonal preserving, as claimed.
∎

A.1.
What about outsplitting?

The following definition was introduced in
[
Lis24
, Definition 1.5.1]
.

Definition A.4

(2-graph outsplitting)
.

Let
\Lambda
be a row-finite source-free 2-graph. Let
G=(\Lambda^{0},\Lambda^{1},r,s)
be its 1-skeleton. An
outsplitting partition
of
G
is a partition
\mathcal{E}=\{\mathcal{E}_{w}:w\in\Lambda^{0}\}
of
\Lambda^{\epsilon_{1}}\sqcup\Lambda^{\epsilon_{2}}
, where
\mathcal{E}_{w}=\{\mathcal{E}_{w}^{1},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{w}^{m(w)}\}
is a partition of
s^{-1}(w)\cap\Lambda^{1}
satisfying the following analogue of the pairing condition:

\text{ if }f,g\in s^{-1}(w)\cap\Lambda^{1}\text{ and }\exists a,b\in G^{1}\text{ s.t. }af\sim bg,\text{ then }f\in\mathcal{E}_{w}^{j}\iff g\in\mathcal{E}_{w}^{j}.

We then define a 2-colored graph
((\Lambda_{O}^{0},\Lambda_{O}^{1},r_{O},s_{O}),d)
where

•

\Lambda_{O}^{0}=\{v^{j}:v\in\Lambda^{0},1\leq j\leq m(v)\},

•

\Lambda_{O}^{\epsilon_{i}}=\{f^{j}:f\in\Lambda^{\epsilon_{i}},1\leq j\leq m(r(f))\},

•

s_{\Lambda_{O}}(f^{j})=s(f)^{k}
if
f\in\mathcal{E}_{s(f)}^{k},

•

r_{\Lambda_{O}}(f^{j})=r(f)^{j}
,

•

d(f^{j})=d(f).

The commuting squares in
\Lambda_{O}
are given by

f^{i}g^{j}\sim_{O}a^{i}b^{k}\iff fg\sim ab,f\in\mathcal{E}^{j}_{r(g)},a\in\mathcal{E}^{k}_{r(b)}.

An argument analogous to Theorem
5.10
(cf. also
[
Lis24
, Claim 1.5.1]
) will show that
\Lambda_{O}
is a 2-graph.

In general, by
[
Lis24
, Theorem 4.0.1]
, 2-graph outsplitting results in a stable isomorphism of
C^{*}
-algebras but not an on-the-nose isomorphism
C^{*}(\Lambda)\cong C^{*}(\Lambda_{O})
.
Therefore, by
[
CR21
, Theorem 3.2]
, outsplitting (even for directed graphs; see
[
BP04
, Example 5.1]
) does not give rise to an eventual conjugacy, and in particular does not give rise to a conjugacy. In particular, we can’t expect to describe 2-graph outsplitting in terms of Johnson–Madden outsplitting, or other related moves.
